during the partial packing procedure, are not counted by any edge tester.
However, our intuition is that the number of such edges only yields a minor order contribution, and in fact, we can employ
\mathbf{S}(r)
(d)
to also account for those edges.
We make the following observation

\displaystyle\left|E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{g},J,J_{X},J_{V})\right|

(6.29)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \leq\penalty\ \sum_{\mathscrs{p}}\omega_{\mathscrs{p}}\left(\textstyle\bigsqcup_{j\in J}E(\mathcal{B}_{j}^{r})\right)+\sum_{j\in J}\left|\left\{\mathscrs{e}\in E(\mathcal{H}[\mathscr{X}_{\cup{\mathscrs{r}}}])\colon\phi_{r}^{+}(\mathscrs{e}\cap\mathscr{X}_{\cup(\{j\}\cup(\mathscrs{r}\setminus J))})\subseteq\mathscrs{g}\right\}\right|.

We obtain an upper bound on the first term in (
6.29
) by employing
\mathbf{S}(r)
(b)
as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathscrs{p}}\omega_{\mathscrs{p}}\left(\textstyle\bigsqcup_{j\in J}E(\mathcal{B}_{j}^{r})\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\text{$\mathbf{S}(r)$\ref{edge weight}}}}}{{\leq}}\sum_{\mathscrs{p}}\left(\left(\mathbbm{1}\{J_{XV}=\emptyset\}+{\varepsilon}_{T}\right)d_{B}^{-1}\prod_{j\in J}d_{j}\cdot\frac{\omega_{\iota,\mathscrs{p}}(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{\mathscrs{r}}})}{n^{k-|J|}}+n^{{\varepsilon}_{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hphantom{{\text{$\mathbf{S}(r)$\ref{edge weight}}}}}}{{\leq}}\left(\mathbbm{1}\{J_{XV}=\emptyset\}+{\varepsilon}_{T}\right)d_{B}^{-1}\prod_{j\in J}d_{j}\cdot\frac{\sum_{\mathscrs{p}}|E_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathscrs{p},\mathscrs{r},J)|}{n^{k-|J|}}+n^{2{\varepsilon}_{T}}

(6.30)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hphantom{{\text{$\mathbf{S}(r)$\ref{edge weight}}}}}}{{\leq}}\left(\mathbbm{1}\{J_{XV}=\emptyset\}+{\varepsilon}_{T}\right)\gamma^{-1}n^{|J|}\prod_{j\in J}d_{j},

where we used that
\sum_{\mathscrs{p}}|E_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathscrs{p},\mathscrs{r},J)|\leq d_{A}n^{k}
and
d_{B}^{-1}d_{A}=(\gamma d)^{-1}(1-\gamma)d\leq\gamma^{-1}
.

An upper bound on the second term in (
6.29
) can be obtained by employing
\mathbf{S}(r)
(d)

(6.31)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in J}\left|\left\{\mathscrs{e}\in E(\mathcal{H}[\mathscr{X}_{\cup{\mathscrs{r}}}])\colon\phi_{r}^{+}(\mathscrs{e}\cap\mathscr{X}_{\cup(\{j\}\cup(\mathscrs{r}\setminus J))})\subseteq\mathscrs{g}\right\}\right|\leq\sum_{j\in J}n^{k-|\{j\}\cup(\mathscrs{r}\setminus J)|+2{\varepsilon}_{T}}\leq n^{|J|-1+3{\varepsilon}_{T}}.

Substituting (
6.30

) in (

.

Step 5.3.

Embedding one
H\in\mathcal{H}

.
Suppose we are given

for all

\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)
and

, let

\displaystyle E{r}^{bad}

\displaystyle:=\big\{\mathscrs{e}\in E(H[X_{\cup\mathscrs{r}}^{H}])\colon|\mathscrs{e}\cap Y_{\cup\mathscrs{r}}^{H}|\geq 2\big\};

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt\mathcal{E}^{bad}:=\textstyle\bigcup_{\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)}E{r}^{bad};\penalty\ \quad Y_{i}^{bad}

\displaystyle:=\{\mathscrs{e}\cap Y_{i}^{H}\colon\mathscrs{e}\in\mathcal{E}^{bad}\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{good}

\displaystyle:=\{\mathscrs{e}\in E(H)\colon|\mathscrs{e}\cap Y_{\cup[r]}^{H}|=1\};

\displaystyle\hskip-9.95863pt\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ Y_{i}^{good}:=Y_{i}^{H}\setminus Y_{i}^{bad}.\quad\quad\penalty\ \penalty\ \hphantom{Y_{i}^{bad}}

Claim 4.

Suppose
G^{\circ}\subseteq G_{B}
,
H\in\mathcal{H}
, and
Y_{i}^{H}\subseteq X_{i}^{H}
for all

(A)

6

and

;

(B)
C

|E{r}^{bad}|\leq\mu^{3/2}n
for all

(K)

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbb{P}}\left[\phi^{H}(\mathscrs{e}\cap X_{\cup I}^{H})=\{v_{i}\}_{i\in I}\right]\leq{\prod_{i\in I\colon\mathscrs{e}\cap Y_{i}^{H}\neq\emptyset}2(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}}

for all

, index sets

Proof of claim:
We split the proof of Claim
6
into two parts, Step
6
and
6
.
In Step
6
, we greedily embed all the
H
-edges of
\mathcal{E}^{bad}
into
G_{B}-G^{\circ}
by considering the clusters in turn.
Afterwards, in Step
6
, we are left with the
H
-edges in
\mathcal{E}^{good}
; that is, where only a single vertex is not yet embedded.
The assumptions
(B)C
and
(C)C
will guarantee that we only used few edges in
G_{B}-G^{\circ}
in Step
6
, such that we merely have to modify our candidacy graphs.
Hence, we can easily find a perfect matching in each of these candidacy graphs to embed the
H
-edges of
\mathcal{E}^{good}
, which will complete the embedding of
H
.
This matching procedure can be performed independently for each cluster as the
H
-edges in
\mathcal{E}^{good}
only contain a single vertex that is not yet embedded.
Clearly, this approach will establish
(H)C
and
(J)C
.

In both steps, we will embed the vertices of
H
by a random procedure in order to establish
(K)C
.
We will do so by making several random choices sequentially which naturally yields a probability distribution.
Since some of these choices lead to instances that do not yield a valid or good embedding, we will discard some of these instances and say the random procedure terminates with failure in these cases.
We will show that the proportion of choices with failure instances is exponentially small, that is, the probability that the random procedure terminates with failure is exponentially small in
n
.
This allows us to discard some of these choices / instances and to restrict our probability space to the remaining ‘nice’ outcomes.
Since the failure probability is exponentially small in
n
, this does not have a significant effect on the probability in
(K)C
.

Step 5.4.

– Embedding the
H
-edges in
\mathcal{E}^{bad}

(See (

Note that

Hence,
S{r}
only serves as a ‘dummy’
(k-1)
-set for updating the candidacy graph
B_{q+1}^{H}
conveniently and to artificially restrict the candidates for
y
.
That is,
V_{q+1}\cap N_{G_{B}}(\mathcal{S}_{y}\setminus\{S{r}\})
are also suitable candidates where we could embed
y
, assuming that
\mathscrs{e}\setminus\{y\}
has not been embedded yet.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{y}^{dummy}
be the set of all these
(k-1)
-sets
S{r}
for
y
, and note that
|\mathcal{S}_{y}^{dummy}|=b_{q}(y)
.

During our process of embedding the
H
-edges in
\mathcal{E}^{bad}
, we will have to drop this artificial restriction of the candidate sets.
To that end, suppose we are given a spanning subgraph
B\subseteq B_{q+1}^{H}
,
y\in Y_{q+1}^{bad}
, and there exists a set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{y}
of
(k-1)
-sets such that we can write

\displaystyle N_{B}(y)=V_{q+1}\cap N_{G_{B}-G^{\circ}}(\mathcal{S}_{y}^{\prime}).

Then let
B^{\ominus dummy}
be the spanning supergraph of
B
where the neighbourhood of each vertex
y\in Y_{q+1}^{bad}
is given by

(6.33)

\displaystyle N_{B^{\ominus dummy}}(y)=V_{q+1}\cap N_{G_{B}-G^{\circ}}(\mathcal{S}_{y}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{y}^{dummy}).

We inductively prove that the following statement
\mathbf{C1}(q)
holds for all
q\in[r]_{0}
, which will extend
\phi_{r}|_{V(H)\setminus V(\widetilde{H})}
by embedding the edges in
\mathcal{E}^{bad}
into
G_{B}-G^{\circ}
.
To that end, we define a set of good pairs of vertices.

(6.34)

For every
i\in[r]
, let
Y_{i}^{good\penalty\ pairs}\subseteq\binom{Y_{i}^{good}}{2}
be the set containing all pairs
\{y,y^{\prime}\}
with
\mathcal{S}_{y}\cap\mathcal{S}_{y^{\prime}}=\emptyset
where
\mathcal{S}_{x}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)\colon i\in\mathscrs{r}}V_{\sqcup\mathscrs{r}\setminus\{i\}}
is such that
N_{B_{i}^{H}}(x)=V_{i}\cap N_{G_{B}}(\mathcal{S}_{x})
for each
x\in\{y,y^{\prime}\}
.

We note for future reference that

(6.35)

\displaystyle\bigg|\binom{Y_{i}^{good}}{2}\setminus Y_{i}^{good\penalty\ pairs}\bigg|\leq 2n\cdot n^{1/4+{\varepsilon}_{T}}\leq n^{4/3},

since
B_{i}^{H}
is
({\varepsilon}_{T},t^{2/3})
-well-intersecting as defined in (
4.2
).

\mathbf{C1}(q)
.

There exists a probability distribution of the injective functions
\phi_{q}^{bad}\colon Y^{bad}_{\cup[q]}\to W^{H}_{\cup[q]}
with
\phi^{\prime}_{q}:=\phi_{q}^{bad}\cup\phi_{r}|_{V(H)\setminus V(\widetilde{H})}
such that

(I)
C
6

\phi^{\prime}_{q}
is an embedding of
H^{\prime}_{q}:=H[Y_{\cup[q]}^{bad}\cup(V(H)\setminus V(\widetilde{H}))]
into
G
;

(II)
C
6

all edges in
H[Y_{\cup[q]}^{bad}\cup(V(H)\setminus V(\widetilde{H}))]
that contain a vertex in
Y_{\cup[q]}^{bad}
are embedded on an edge in
G_{B}-G^{\circ}
;

(III)
C
6

for every vertex
x\in Y_{i}^{good}
and
i\in[q]
, there are at most
\mu^{4/3}d_{i}n
vertices
y\in Y_{i}^{bad}
with
\phi_{q}^{bad}(y)\in N_{B_{i}^{H}}(x)
;

(IV)
C
6

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbb{P}}\left[\phi_{q}^{\prime}(\mathscrs{e}\cap X_{\cup I}^{H})=\{v_{i}\}_{i\in I}\right]\leq{\prod_{i\in I\colon\mathscrs{e}\cap Y_{i}^{H}\neq\emptyset}2(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}}

for all
\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)
,
m\in[k]
,
I\in\binom{\mathscrs{r}\cap[q]}{m}
,
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in V_{\sqcup I}
and
\mathscrs{e}\in E{r}^{bad}
.

The statement
\mathbf{C1}(0)
clearly holds for
\phi_{0}^{bad}
being the empty function.
Hence, we assume the truth of
\mathbf{C1}(q)
for some
q\in[r-1]_{0}
.
Our strategy to establish
\mathbf{C1}(q+1)
is as follows.
We extend the probability space given in
\mathbf{C1}(q)
by making further random choices.
For
\phi_{q}^{bad}
as in
\mathbf{C1}(q)
,
we aim to find a matching
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}\colon Y_{q+1}^{bad}\to W^{H}_{q+1}
in
a suitable candidacy graph between
Y_{q+1}^{bad}
and
W^{H}_{q+1}

that extends
\phi_{q}^{bad}
to
\phi_{q+1}^{bad}:=\phi_{q}^{bad}\cup\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}
.
If we can find such a matching
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}
, then
\mathbf{C1}(q+1)
(I)C
and
(II)C
will hold by the definition of this suitable candidacy graph.
In particular, we will find
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}
by a random procedure to also ensure
(IV)C
.
We will discard an exponentially small proportion of random choices during this procedure in order to satisfy
(III)C
and to obtain a suitable embedding
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}
.

Let us describe this suitable candidacy graph.
We will choose
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}
randomly in

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}:=(B_{q+1}^{H})^{G^{\circ}}(\phi_{q}^{bad})^{\ominus dummy}[Y_{q+1}^{bad},W^{H}_{q+1}].

That is,
\widetilde{B}
arises from
B_{q+1}^{H}
as follows.

•

First, we restrict the candidate sets in
B_{q+1}^{H}
to those edges whose corresponding edges in
G_{B}
have not been used in
G^{\circ}
for packing graphs
H_{1},\ldots,H_{h}
in previous rounds.

•

Second, we restrict the candidate sets with respect to the packing
\phi_{q}^{bad}
of the vertices in
Y_{\cup[q]}^{bad}
according to (
6.32
).

•

Third, we drop the restriction of the candidate sets to the dummy
(k-1)
-sets as in (
6.33
).

•

In the end, we consider the induced subgraph of this candidacy graph on the bad vertices
Y_{q+1}^{bad}
and the vertices
W_{q+1}^{H}
that can be used for the completion.

For the sake of a better readability, let
B:=(B_{q+1}^{H})^{G^{\circ}}
.

To guarantee the existence of
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}
, we will show that the degree of every vertex
y\in Y_{q+1}^{bad}
is sufficiently large in
B
and also in
\widetilde{B}
.
Let
y\in Y_{q+1}^{bad}
be fixed.
Since
B_{q+1}^{H}
is
({\varepsilon}_{T},t^{2/3})
-well-intersecting by
\mathbf{S}(r)
(a)
, there exists a set
\mathcal{S}_{y}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)\colon q+1\in\mathscrs{r}}V_{\sqcup\mathscrs{r}\setminus\{q+1\}}
of
(k-1)
-sets with
|\mathcal{S}_{y}|\leq t^{2/3}
such that
N_{B_{q+1}^{H}}(y)=V_{q+1}\cap N_{G_{B}}(\mathcal{S}_{y})
.
Hence, by the definition of
B=(B_{q+1}^{H})^{G^{\circ}}
in (
6.25
), we have

(6.36)

\displaystyle N_{B}(y)=V_{q+1}\cap N_{G_{B}-G^{\circ}}(\mathcal{S}_{y}),\quad\text{ and }\quad\dg_{B}(y)=(1\pm 2\mu^{1/5})d_{q+1}n,

because
B
is
(\mu^{1/5},d_{q+1})
-super-regular by
(D)C
.
Of course we have to restrict the potential images of
y
according to the vertices we already embedded by
\phi_{q}^{bad}
.
To this end, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{y}^{bad}:=\left\{\phi_{q}^{bad}(\mathscrs{e}\cap Y_{\cup[q]}^{bad})\cup\phi_{r}|_{V(H)\setminus V(\widetilde{H})}(\mathscrs{e}\setminus V(\widetilde{H}))\colon\mathscrs{e}\in\mathcal{E}^{bad},\mathscrs{e}\cap Y_{\cup([r]\setminus[q])}^{bad}=\{y\}\right\}

and note that
|\mathcal{S}_{y}^{bad}|=b_{q}(y)
.
By the definition of the candidacy graph
B(\phi_{q}^{bad})
in (
6.32
) and by (
6.36
), we obtain

\displaystyle N_{B(\phi_{q}^{bad})}(y)=V_{q+1}\cap N_{G_{B}-G^{\circ}}(\mathcal{S}_{y}\cup\mathcal{S}_{y}^{bad}),

and thus,
together with
(F)C
and (
6.36
), we have that

\displaystyle\dg_{B(\phi_{q}^{bad})}(y)=(1\pm\mu^{1/6})d_{q+1}d_{B}^{b_{q}(y)}n.

Since
|\mathcal{S}_{y}^{dummy}|=b_{q}(y)
, this implies

(6.37)

\displaystyle\dg_{B(\phi_{q}^{bad})^{\ominus dummy}}(y)=\Big|V_{q+1}\cap N_{G_{B}-G^{\circ}}\big((\mathcal{S}_{y}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{y}^{dummy})\cup\mathcal{S}_{y}^{bad}\big)\Big|=(1\pm\mu^{1/6})d_{q+1}n.

Now, we obtain

(6.38)

\displaystyle N_{\widetilde{B}}(y)=W^{H}_{q+1}\cap N_{G_{B}-G^{\circ}}\big((\mathcal{S}_{y}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{y}^{dummy})\cup\mathcal{S}_{y}^{bad}\big)

and thus, by
(G)C
and (
6.37
), we have that

(6.39)

\displaystyle\dg_{\widetilde{B}}(y)=(1\pm\mu^{1/7})d_{q+1}\mu n.

In order to guarantee
(IV)C
, we find
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}
via the following random procedure:

•

for every vertex
y\in Y_{q+1}^{bad}
in turn, we choose a neighbour in
N_{\widetilde{B}}(y)
uniformly at random among all neighbours that have not been chosen in previous turns;

•

we terminate the random procedure with failure at some step of the procedure, say at the turn of some vertex
y\in Y_{q+1}^{bad}
,

–

if we have less than
2(\mu d_{q+1}n)/3
choices to select an image for
y
in
N_{\widetilde{B}}(y)
, or

–

if there is a vertex
x\in Y_{q+1}^{good}
such that
(III)C
is violated, that is, we mapped in previous turns already more than
\mu^{4/3}d_{q+1}n
vertices of
Y_{q+1}^{bad}
into
N_{B_{q+1}^{H}}(x)
.

We show in the following claim that this random procedure terminates with failure only with exponentially small probability.
If the procedure does not terminate with failure, we obtain a random
Y_{q+1}^{bad}
-saturating matching
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}\colon Y_{q+1}^{bad}\to W^{H}_{q+1}
in
\widetilde{B}
, which by definition of the candidacy graph
B_{q+1}^{H}
and
\widetilde{B}
implies
\mathbf{C1}(q+1)
(I)C
–
(III)C
.
Further,
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}
satisfies the following.

(6.40)

For all
y\in Y_{q+1}^{bad}
,
w\in W^{H}_{q+1}
, we have that
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}(y)=w
with probability at most
2(\mu d_{q+1}n)^{-1}
.

Hence, the following claim together with
\mathbf{C1}(q)
(IV)C
establishes
\mathbf{C1}(q+1)
(IV)C
.

Claim 5.

The random procedure for computing
\sigma^{bad}_{q+1}
terminates with failure with probability at most
{\rm e}^{-n^{1/2}}
.

Proof of claim:
Let
Y_{q+1}^{bad}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\}
and we consider every vertex in turn, that is,
y_{\ell+1}
will be treated after
y_{\ell}
.
For all
x\in Y_{q+1}^{H}
and
\ell\in[m]
, let
\xi_{\ell}(x)
be the random variable that counts the number of covered neighbours so far, that is, the number of vertices
v\in N_{B_{q+1}^{H}}(x)
such that
\sigma_{q+1}^{bad}(y)=v
for some
y\in\{y_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]}
.
We say the random procedure fails at step
\ell\in[m]
, if
\ell
is the smallest integer such that

•

\xi_{\ell}(z)>\mu^{2/5}d_{q+1}\mu n
for some vertex
z\in Y_{q+1}^{good}\cup\{y_{i}\}_{i\in[m]\setminus[\ell]}
.

We show that the random procedure fails at some step
\ell\in[m]
with probability at most
{\rm e}^{-n^{2/3}}
.
A union bound then establishes Claim
6
.

We fix
\ell\in[m]
and a vertex
z\in Y_{q+1}^{good}\cup\{y_{i}\}_{i\in[m]\setminus[\ell]}
.
By employing (
6.38
), (
6.39
) and that
B_{q+1}^{H}
is
({\varepsilon}_{T},t^{2/3})
-well-intersecting, we obtain

(6.41)

\displaystyle|N_{\widetilde{B}}(y\land z)|=(1\pm\mu^{1/7})d_{q+1}^{2}\mu n\text{ for all but at most $n^{1/3}$ vertices $y\in\{y_{i}\}_{i\in[\ell]}$.}

Further, at the turn of each
y_{i}
,
i\in[\ell]
, we have at least

(6.42)

\displaystyle|N_{\widetilde{B}}(y_{i})|-\mu^{2/5}d_{q+1}\mu n\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:B'-degree y}}}{{\geq}}(1-2\mu^{1/7})d_{q+1}\mu n

choices for the embedding of
y_{i}
.
Hence, by (
6.41
) and (
6.42
), the probability that a vertex
y_{i}
,
i\in[\ell]
which satisfies (
6.41
) is mapped into
N_{\widetilde{B}}(z)
is at most

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\mu^{1/7})d_{q+1}^{2}\mu n}{(1-2\mu^{1/7})d_{q+1}\mu n}\leq 2d_{q+1}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi_{\ell}(z)\right]\leq 2d_{q+1}\ell+n^{1/3}\leq 2d_{q+1}|Y_{q+1}^{bad}|+n^{1/3}\leq 3d_{q+1}\alpha^{-1}\mu^{3/2}n,

where we used that
|Y_{q+1}^{bad}|\leq\alpha^{-1}\mu^{3/2}n
by
(B)C
.
Hence, Theorem
3.1
implies that
\xi_{\ell}(z)>\mu^{2/5}d_{q+1}\mu n
with probability, say, at most
{\rm e}^{-n^{2/3}}
for some
z\in Y_{q+1}^{good}\cup\{y_{i}\}_{i\in[m]\setminus[\ell]}
.
Thus, the random procedure fails at step
\ell
with probability at most
{\rm e}^{-n^{2/3}}
.
A simple union bound completes the proof of Claim
6
.

-

Step 5.5.

Proof of Claim
6
– Embedding the
H
-edges in
\mathcal{E}^{good}

Let
\phi_{r}^{bad}\colon Y^{bad}_{\cup[r]}\to W^{H}_{\cup[r]}
and
\phi_{r}^{\prime}=\phi_{r}^{bad}\cup\phi_{r}|_{V(H)\setminus V(\widetilde{H})}
be as in
\mathbf{C1}(r)
obtained in Step
6
.
Recall that for all
i\in[r]
, we have
Y_{i}^{good}=Y_{i}^{H}\setminus Y_{i}^{bad}
and let
W_{i}^{good}:=W_{i}^{H}\setminus\phi_{r}^{bad}(Y_{i}^{bad})
, and thus clearly,
|Y_{i}^{good}|=|W_{i}^{good}|
.
For every
i\in[r]
, we aim to embed the vertices
Y_{i}^{good}
onto
W_{i}^{good}
by finding a perfect matching in
(B_{i}^{H})^{G^{\circ}}
.

Let
i\in[r]
be fixed.
By
(E)C
, we have that
\widehat{B}_{i}:=(B_{i}^{H})^{G^{\circ}}[Y_{i}^{H},W_{i}^{H}]
is
(\mu^{1/6},d_{i})
-super-regular. We show that for every vertex in
Y_{i}^{good}\cup W_{i}^{good}
, we only removed few incident edges when take the subgraph
B_{i}^{good}:=\widehat{B}_{i}[Y_{i}^{good},W_{i}^{good}]
of
\widehat{B}_{i}
.
For a vertex
v\in W_{i}^{good}
, we removed at most
\mu^{3/2}d_{i}n
incident edges by
(C)C
.
For a vertex
x\in Y_{i}^{good}
, we removed at most
\mu^{4/3}d_{i}n
incident edges by
(III)C
.
Hence, by employing Fact
3.4
, we obtain that
B_{i}^{good}
is
(\mu^{1/19},d_{i})
-super-regular for every
i\in[r]
.

Our strategy is to apply Lemma
3.7
that allows us to find a regular spanning subgraph of
B_{i}^{good}
from which we can easily take a random perfect matching.
In order to satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
3.7
, we show that the common neighbourhood of most of the pairs in
Y_{i}^{good}
is also not too large in
B_{i}^{good}
.
To that end, we fix a pair of vertices
\{y,y^{\prime}\}\in Y_{i}^{good\penalty\ pairs}
as defined in (
6.34
) and let
\mathcal{S}_{y},\mathcal{S}_{y^{\prime}}
be the sets of
(k-1)
-sets such that
N_{B_{i}^{H}}(x)=V_{i}\cap N_{G_{B}}(\mathcal{S}_{x})
for each
x\in\{y,y^{\prime}\}
.
We have that

(6.43)

\displaystyle N_{\widehat{B}_{i}}(y\land y^{\prime})=W_{i}^{H}\cap N_{(B_{i}^{H})^{G^{\circ}}}(y\land y^{\prime})=W_{i}^{H}\cap N_{G_{B}-G^{\circ}}(\mathcal{S}_{y}\cup\mathcal{S}_{y^{\prime}}).

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle|N_{B_{i}^{good}}(y\land y^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hphantom{\eqref{eq:common neighbourhood tB_i},\ref{item:mu well-intersecting}}}}{{\leq}}|N_{\widehat{B}_{i}}(y\land y^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:common neighbourhood tB_i},\ref{item:mu well-intersecting}}}{{\leq}}(1+{\varepsilon}_{T}^{1/2})\mu\big|V_{i}\cap N_{G_{B}-G^{\circ}}(\mathcal{S}_{y}\cup\mathcal{S}_{y^{\prime}})\big|

(6.44)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \ref{item:G_B-Gcirc typical}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ }}{{\leq}}(1+2\mu^{1/2})\mu d_{i}^{2}n,

where we used for the last equality that
\mathcal{S}_{y}\cap\mathcal{S}_{y^{\prime}}=\emptyset
since
\{y,y^{\prime}\}\in Y_{i}^{good\penalty\ pairs}
, and thus,
d_{B}^{|\mathcal{S}_{y}\cup\mathcal{S}_{y^{\prime}}|}=d_{i}^{2}
.
Hence, (
6.35
) implies that all but at most
n^{4/3}
pairs of vertices in
Y_{i}^{good}
satisfy (
6.44
).

Finally, we can apply Lemma
3.7
and obtain a spanning
\mu d_{i}n/2
-regular subgraph of
B_{i}^{good}
.
In particular,
B_{i}^{good}
contains
\mu d_{i}n/2
edge-disjoint perfect matchings, from which we choose one perfect matching
\sigma_{i}^{good}\colon Y_{i}^{good}\to W_{i}^{good}
for each
i\in[r]
uniformly and independently at random.
We crucially observe:

(6.45)

For all
i\in[r]
,
y_{i}\in Y_{i}^{good}
,
w_{i}\in W_{i}^{good}
, we have that
\sigma_{i}^{good}(y_{i})=w_{i}
with probability at most
2(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}
.

Further, since all the vertices in
H_{\ast}[Y_{\cup[r]}^{good}]
are isolated, we have that
\phi^{good}:=\bigcup_{i\in[r]}\sigma_{i}^{good}
is an injective function
\phi^{good}\colon Y_{\cup[r]}^{good}\to W_{\cup[r]}^{good}
such that
\phi_{r}|_{V(H)\setminus V(\widetilde{H})}\cup\phi^{good}
is a random packing of
H[Y_{\cup[q]}^{good}\cup(V(H)\setminus V(\widetilde{H}))]
into
G
that embeds all edges in
H[Y_{\cup[r]}^{good}\cup(V(H)\setminus V(\widetilde{H}))]
that contain a vertex in
Y_{\cup[r]}^{good}
on an edge in
G_{B}-G^{\circ}
.
In particular, since we find the perfect matchings in the candidacy graphs
(B_{i}^{H})^{G^{\circ}}
induced on
Y_{i}^{good}\cup W_{i}^{good}
for each
i\in[r]
, we have for
\phi^{H}:=\phi_{r}|_{V(H)\setminus V(\widetilde{H})}\cup\phi_{r}^{bad}\cup\phi^{good}
that

•

\phi^{H}
is an embedding of
H
into
G

where
\phi^{H}(X_{i}^{H})=V_{i}
for all
i\in[r]
, which establishes
(H)C
;

•

all
H
-edges that contain a vertex in
Y_{\cup[r]}^{H}
are embedded on an edge in
G_{B}-G^{\circ}
, which establishes
(J)C
;

•

for all
m\in[k]
, index sets
I\in\binom{[r]}{m}
,
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in V_{\sqcup I}
, and
H
-edges
\mathscrs{e}
that contain a vertex in
Y_{\cup I}^{H}
, we have that
\phi^{H}(\mathscrs{e}\cap X_{\cup I}^{H})=\{v_{i}\}_{i\in I}
with probability at most
{\prod_{i\in I\colon\mathscrs{e}\cap Y_{i}^{H}\neq\emptyset}2(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}}
by
\mathbf{C1}(r)
(IV)C
and (
6.45
), which establishes
(K)C
.

This completes the proof of Claim
6
.

-

Step 5.6.

The random packing procedure – Claim
6

We now proceed to our Random Packing Procedure (RPP).
Let
G_{0}^{\circ}
be the edgeless graph on
V(G)
and let
\phi^{0}
be the empty function.
We perform the following random procedure.

Random Packing Procedure (RPP)

For
h=1,\ldots,|\mathcal{H}|
do:

•

Set
H:=H_{h}
.
For all
i\in[r]
, independently activate every vertex in
\widehat{X}_{i}^{H}
with probability
\mu
and let
Y_{i}^{H}
be the union of
X_{i}^{H}\setminus\widehat{X}_{i}^{H}
and all activated vertices in
\widehat{X}_{i}^{H}
.

•

If the assumptions of Claim
6
are satisfied, apply Claim
6
and obtain a random packing
\phi^{H}
that satisfies
(H)C
–
(K)C
; otherwise terminate with failure.

•

Set
\phi^{h}:=\phi^{h-1}\cup\phi^{H}
and
G_{h}^{\circ}:=G_{h-1}^{\circ}\cup(\phi^{H}(H)\cap G_{B})
.

Claim 6.

With probability at least
1-1/n
, the RPP terminates without failure and satisfies conclusion
(iii)
of Lemma
6.1
.

Proof of claim:
We prove Claim
6
in Steps
6
–
6
.
Our general strategy is to guarantee that we can apply Claim
6
in each turn of the procedure.
To that end, we will introduce a collection of random variables that we call
identifiers
.
Such an identifier indicates an unlikely event and if this event happens, we say the identifier
detects alarm
and we simply terminate the RPP with failure.
This means, if an identifier detects alarm at some turn
h\in[|\mathcal{H}|]
, we terminate the RPP and deactivate all further identifiers; that is, the probability that they detect alarm is set to
0
.
We show that the probability that an individual identifier detects alarm is exponentially small in
n
.
In the end, a union bound over all identifiers will imply that with probability at least
1-1/n
, none of the identifiers will detect alarm and thus, the RPP terminates without failure.

For most of the identifiers, it follows by a standard application of Theorem
3.1
that the probability to detect alarm is exponentially small (in fact, often Chernoff’s inequality suffices).
To that end, in the subsequent Steps
6
–
6
, we often describe only the random variables to which we apply Theorem
3.1
.

First, let us observe that
\mathbf{S}(r)
implies that

(6.46)

\displaystyle\big|X_{i}^{H}\setminus\widehat{X}_{i}^{H}\big|=\big|V_{i}\setminus\phi_{r}(\widehat{X}_{i}^{H})\big|\leq 2{\varepsilon}_{T}n\text{ for all $H\in\mathcal{H}$, $i\in[r]$.}

Further, for
Y_{i}^{H}
as in the RPP, let
W_{i}^{H}:=V_{i}\setminus\phi_{r}(\widehat{X}_{i}^{H}\setminus Y_{i}^{H})
.
For convenience, we also call a vertex
w\in W_{i}^{H}\cap\phi_{r}(\widehat{X}_{i}^{H})

activated
by
H\in\mathcal{H}
. (Recall that we only activate vertices in
\widehat{X}_{i}^{H}
.)

Step 5.7.

Proof of Claim
6
– Establishing
(A)C
–
(G)C

In this step, in order to establish
(A)C
–
(G)C
at each turn of the RPP, we consider several random variables for which we individually introduce an identifier that detects alarm if the considered random variable is not within a factor of
(1\pm{\varepsilon})
of its expectation.
As mentioned above, for each identifier a standard application of Theorem
3.1
implies that the probability to detect alarm is exponentially small, say,
{\rm e}^{-n^{1/2}}
.
Let us only describe the random variables that we consider for establishing
(A)C
–
(G)C
.

To establish
(A)C
, for each
H\in\mathcal{H}
,
i\in[r]
, we consider the sum of indicator variables which indicate whether a vertex is activated in
X_{i}^{H}
.
Together with (
6.46
), this implies
(A)C
.

To establish
(B)C
, for each
H\in\mathcal{H}
and
\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)
, we consider the random variable that counts how many
H
-edges
\mathscrs{e}
lie in
X_{\cup\mathscrs{r}}^{H}
where at least two vertices of
\mathscrs{e}
are activated; in view of the statement, we may assume that
e_{H}(X_{\cup\mathscrs{r}}^{H})\geq\mu^{3/2}n
.
Note that the probability for an edge
\mathscrs{e}
that at least two vertices are activated is at most
k^{2}\mu^{2}
.
Together with (
6.46
), this implies
(B)C
.

To establish
(C)C
, for all
H\in\mathcal{H}
,
i\in[r]
, and
v\in V_{i}
, we consider the random variable
\xi
that counts how many
B_{i}^{H}
-neighbours of
v
in
X_{i}^{H}
are activated and lie in an
H
-edge
\mathscrs{e}
where a second vertex in
\mathscrs{e}
is either activated or left unembedded.
With Claim
6
we have that

\mathrm{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi\right]\leq 2k^{2}\mu^{2}\alpha^{-1}d_{i}n+2\mu{\varepsilon}_{T}^{1/2}d_{i}n\leq\mu^{5/3}d_{i}n
.

Together with Claim
6
, this implies
(C)C
.

To establish
(G)C
, for all
i\in[r]
,
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)\colon i\in\mathscrs{r}}V_{\sqcup\mathscrs{r}\setminus\{i\}}
,
|\mathcal{S}|\leq t
, we consider the sum of indicator variables which each indicates whether a vertex in
\phi_{r}(\widehat{X}_{i}^{H})\cap N_{G_{B}-G_{h}^{\circ}}(\mathcal{S})
is activated.
By
\mathbf{S}(r)
(e)
, we further have that

(6.47)

\displaystyle\big|(V_{i}\setminus\phi_{r}(\widehat{X}_{i}^{H}))\cap N_{G_{B}}(\mathcal{S})\big|\leq{\varepsilon}_{T}\big|V_{i}\cap N_{G_{B}}(\mathcal{S})\big|.

Altogether, this implies
(G)C
.

In order to establish
(D)C
–
(F)C
, we claim that if no identifier detected alarm until turn
h\in[|\mathcal{H}|]
, then for all
i\in[r]

(6.48)

\displaystyle\left|\textstyle\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}}\left(V_{i}\cap N_{G^{\circ}_{h}}(S)\cap N_{G_{B}}(\mathcal{S})\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mu^{2/3}d_{B}^{|\mathcal{S}|}n\text{ for all $\mathcal{S}\subseteq\textstyle\bigcup_{\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)\colon i\in\mathscrs{r}}V_{\sqcup\mathscrs{r}\setminus\{i\}}$, $|\mathcal{S}|\leq t$;}

(6.49)

\displaystyle\rho^{H}_{G^{\circ}_{h}}(v)

\displaystyle\leq\mu^{2/3}d_{i}n\text{ for all $H\in\mathcal{H}$, $v\in V_{i}$,}

where
\rho^{H}_{G^{\circ}_{h}}(v)
is defined as in (
6.26
).
We verify (
6.48
) and (
6.49
) in the subsequent Steps
6
and
6
, respectively, and first establish
(D)C
–
(F)C
assuming (
6.48
) and (
6.49
).

To establish
(F)C
, recall that
G_{B}
is
(2{\varepsilon}_{0},t,d_{B})
-typical with respect to
R
by (
6.4
).
Hence, we obtain from (
6.48
) and the definition of typicality that
G_{B}-G^{\circ}_{h}
is
(\mu^{1/2},t,d_{B})
-typical with respect to
R
, which implies
(F)C
.

To establish
(D)C
for
H=H_{h+1}
, recall that
B_{i}^{H}
is
({\varepsilon}_{T},d_{i})
-super-regular and
({\varepsilon}_{T},t^{2/3})
-well-intersecting with respect to
G_{B}
for all
i\in[r]
.
Observe that there exists a set
\mathcal{S}_{x}
for every
x\in X_{i}^{H}
with
|\mathcal{S}_{x}|\leq t^{2/3}
and
N_{B_{i}^{H}}(x)=V_{i}\cap N_{G_{B}}(\mathcal{S}_{x})
.
By the definition of
(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}
in (
6.25
), we have that

N_{(B_{i}^{H})^{G^{\circ}_{h}}}(x)=V_{i}\cap N_{G_{B}-G^{\circ}_{h}}(\mathcal{S}_{x})
.
This together with (
6.48
), and (
6.49
) together with (
6.27
), implies that we removed at most
\mu^{2/3}d_{i}n
edges incident to every vertex to obtain
(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}
from
B_{i}^{H}
.
Now Fact
3.4
yields that
(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}
is
(\mu^{1/5},d_{i})
-super-regular.
This implies
(D)C
.

To establish
(E)C
for
H=H_{h+1}
,
we exploit that
(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}
is
(\mu^{1/5},d_{i})
-super-regular and only have to show that every vertex in
(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}[Y_{i}^{H},W_{i}^{H}]
has the appropriate degree.
To that end, we consider the following random variables for all
x\in X_{i}^{H}
,
v\in V_{i}
,
i\in[r]
.
For
x
, we consider the random variable that counts the number of activated vertices by
H
in
N_{(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}}(x)
, and by employing (
6.47
) and that
B_{i}^{H}
is
({\varepsilon}_{T},t)
-well-intersecting with respect to
G_{B}
, we expect that
N_{(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}}(x)\cap W_{i}^{H}
has size
\mu|N_{(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}}(x)|\pm 2{\varepsilon}_{T}d_{i}n
.
This yields the appropriate degree for
x
.
For
v
, we consider the random variable that counts the number of activated vertices in
N_{(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}}(v)
, and by employing Claim
6
, we expect that
N_{(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}}(v)\cap Y_{i}^{H}
has size
\mu|N_{(B_{i}^{H})^{G_{h}^{\circ}}}(v)|\pm 2{\varepsilon}_{T}d_{i}n
.
This yields the appropriate degree for
v
.
Altogether this implies
(E)C
.

Step 5.8.

Probability to use a
G_{B}
-edge during the completion

We say an edge
\mathscrs{g}\in E(G_{B})
is
used
during the RPP if there exists a graph
H_{h}\in\mathcal{H}
and an edge
\mathscrs{e}\in H_{h}
such that
\phi^{h}(\mathscrs{e})=\mathscrs{g}
.
In this step we show the following claim that we will apply to establish (
6.48
) and (
6.49
) in Steps
6
and
6
.

Claim 7.

For every edge
\mathscrs{g}\in E(G_{B})
, the probability that
\mathscrs{g}
is used during the RPP is at most
\mu^{3/4}
.

Proof of claim:
Let
\mathscrs{g}\in E(G_{B})
and
\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)
with
\mathscrs{g}=\{v_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscrs{r}}\in E(G_{B}[V_{\cup\mathscrs{r}}])
be fixed.
We consider different cases and sets of
\mathcal{H}
-edges that could potentially be embedded onto
\mathscrs{g}
, say in each case with probability at must
\mu^{4/5}
.
In the end, a union bound will establish Claim
6
.
Therefore, for all
m\in[k]
,
J\in\binom{\mathscrs{r}}{m}
, we consider different sets of edges in
\mathcal{H}[\mathscr{X}_{\cup{\mathscrs{r}}}]
where the
m
vertices corresponding to the clusters in
J
are either unembedded or activated and can potentially be mapped onto
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J}
, and where the remaining
k-m
vertices corresponding to the clusters in
\mathscrs{r}\setminus J
have already been embedded onto
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscrs{r}\setminus J}
.
Let
m\in[k]
and
J\in\binom{\mathscrs{r}}{m}
be fixed.

We first consider the set of
H
-edges
\mathscrs{e}=\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscrs{r}}
for all
H\in\mathcal{H}
where no vertex of
\mathscrs{e}
is left unembedded by
\phi_{r}
and for every vertex of
\mathscrs{g}
, there is an
H
-vertex that is mapped onto
\mathscrs{g}
.
That is,
the
m
vertices
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
as well as the
m
vertices
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J}
are activated, the vertices
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
can potentially be mapped onto
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J}
, and
\phi_{r}(\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscrs{r}\setminus J})=\{v_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscrs{r}\setminus J}
.
To that end, we first consider the set
\widetilde{E}:=E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{g},J,J_{X}=\emptyset,J_{V}=\emptyset)\setminus E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{g},J,J_{X}=\emptyset,J_{V}\neq\emptyset)

as defined in (
6.28
).
(Note that we defined this set in (
6.28
) only in the more convenient way that
\mathscrs{g}\cap V_{\cup J_{V}}\subseteq\mathscrs{g}\setminus\phi_{r}(X_{\cup\mathscrs{r}}^{H})
, which is the reason why we remove the set
E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{g},J,J_{X}=\emptyset,J_{V}\neq\emptyset)
from the current consideration.)
For an edge
\mathscrs{e}=\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscrs{r}}\in\widetilde{E}
,
in order that
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
can be mapped onto
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J}
during the completion, it must hold that
the vertices
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
and the vertices
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J}
must become activated.
Since
\phi_{r}(\mathscrs{x})\neq\mathscrs{g}
and
\phi_{r}(\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscrs{r}\setminus J})=\{v_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscrs{r}\setminus J}
, it holds that
\phi_{r}(\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J})\neq\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J}
.
Hence, the probability that
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
and
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J}
become activated is at most
\mu^{m+1}
because every vertex in
\widehat{X}_{i}^{H}
for all
i\in[r]
,
H\in\mathcal{H}
is activated independently with probability
\mu
.
Further, by
(K)C
, activated vertices
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
are mapped onto
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J}
with probability at most
2^{m}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}
.
Altogether, this implies that that the probability that some edge in
\widetilde{E}
is mapped onto
\mathscrs{g}
is at most

\displaystyle\mu^{m+1}2^{m}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}\big|E_{\phi_{r}}\left(\mathscrs{g},J,J_{X}=\emptyset,J_{V}=\emptyset\right)\big|

(6.50)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Claim\penalty\ \ref{claim:leftover for g}}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\mu^{m+1}2^{m+1}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}\cdot\gamma^{-1}n^{m}\prod_{i\in J}d_{i}\leq\mu^{4/5},

where we used for the last inequality that
\mu\ll\gamma
.

Next, we consider the sets
E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{g},J,J_{X},J_{V})

where
J_{XV}=J_{X}\cup J_{V}
is non-empty for disjoint
J_{X},J_{V}\subseteq J
.
For an edge
\mathscrs{e}=\{x_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscrs{r}}\in E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{g},J,J_{X},J_{V})
,
the vertices
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
are mapped onto
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J}
with probability at most
2^{m}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}
by
(K)C
.
This implies that the probability that some edge in
E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{g},J,J_{X}=\emptyset,J_{V})
is mapped onto
\mathscrs{g}
is at most

\displaystyle 2^{m}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}\big|E_{\phi_{r}}\left(\mathscrs{g},J,J_{X},J_{V}\right)\big|

(6.51)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Claim\penalty\ \ref{claim:leftover for g}}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle 2^{m}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}\cdot{\varepsilon}_{T}^{1/2}n^{m}\prod_{i\in J}d_{i}\leq{\varepsilon}_{T}^{1/3}.

Now, Claim
6
is established by a union bound over all
m\in[k]
,
J\in\binom{\mathscrs{r}}{m}
, and all possible sets
J_{X},J_{V}
that we considered to be fixed in (
6.50
) and (
6.51
).

-

Step 5.9.

Proof of Claim
6
– Bound in (
6.48
)

We use Claim
6
to verify the claimed bound in (
6.48
).
We fix
i\in[r]
,
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)\colon i\in\mathscrs{r}}V_{\sqcup\mathscrs{r}\setminus\{i\}}

with
|\mathcal{S}|\leq t
and
S\in\mathcal{S}
.
By Claim
6
, each
G_{B}
-edge is used with probability at most
\mu^{3/4}
during the RPP, and thus, by an application of Lemma
3.2
, we have that

|V_{i}\cap N_{G^{\circ}_{h}}(S)\cap N_{G_{B}}(\mathcal{S})|\leq\mu^{7/10}d_{B}^{|\mathcal{S}|}n
with probability at least, say,
1-{\rm e}^{-n^{3/4}}
.
Otherwise we detect alarm.
Together with a union bound this implies the claimed bound in (
6.48
) because
t\cdot\mu^{7/10}d_{B}^{|\mathcal{S}|}n\leq\mu^{2/3}d_{B}^{|\mathcal{S}|}n
.

Step 5.10.

Proof of Claim
6
– Bound for
\rho^{H}_{G^{\circ}_{h}}(v)

We also use Claim
6
to verify the claimed bound for
\rho^{H}_{G^{\circ}_{h}}(v)
in (
6.49
).
(Recall the definition of
\rho_{G^{\circ}_{h}}^{H}(v)
in (
6.26
).)
For all
H\in\mathcal{H}
,
i\in[r]
and
v\in V_{i}
, we have that
v
has at most
(1+2{\varepsilon}_{T})d_{i}n
neighbours in
B_{i}^{H}
.
For each such neighbour
x\in N_{B_{i}^{H}}(v)
, there exists a set
\mathcal{S}_{x}\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{k-1}
with
|\mathcal{S}_{x}|\leq t^{2/3}
such that
N_{B_{i}^{H}}(x)=N_{G_{B}}(\mathcal{S}_{x})
.
Hence, there exist at most
2t^{2/3}d_{i}n
edges
\mathscrs{g}=S\cup\{v\}
in
G_{B}
for some
S\in\bigcup_{x\in N_{B_{i}^{H}}(v)}\mathcal{S}_{x}
.
By Claim
6
, each such
G_{B}
-edge
\mathscrs{g}
is used with probability at most
\mu^{3/4}
during the entire RPP.
We therefore expect that for each
h\in[|\mathcal{H}|]
at most
\mu^{3/4}2t^{2/3}d_{i}n
edges incident to
v
in
B_{i}^{H}
have to be removed when we obtain
(B_{i}^{H})^{G^{\circ}_{h}}
.
Hence, by an application of Lemma
3.2
and a union bound, we obtain that
\rho_{G^{\circ}_{h}}^{H}(v)\leq\mu^{2/3}d_{i}n
for all
h\in[|\mathcal{H}|]
,
H\in\mathcal{H}
,
i\in[r]
,
v\in V_{i}
with probability at least, say,
1-{\rm e}^{-n^{3/4}}
.
Otherwise we detect alarm.
This implies the claimed bound in (
6.49
).

Step 5.11.

Proof of Claim
6
– Establishing
(iii)
of Lemma
6.1

In order to establish conclusion
(iii)
of Lemma
6.1
, let
(\omega,\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}
with centres
\mathscrs{c}=\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
I
be fixed.
We split the proof into two parts, depending on whether we activate a vertex of a tuple
\mathscrs{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}}
that
\phi_{r}
already mapped onto
\mathscrs{c}
, or whether a tuple
\mathscrs{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}}
contains vertices in
\mathcal{X}_{r}\setminus\mathcal{X}_{r}^{\phi_{r}}
and
\mathscrs{x}
is mapped onto
\mathscrs{c}
during the completion.

Let us first consider tuples
\mathscrs{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}}
that we have already embedded onto
\mathscrs{c}
, that is,
\phi_{r}(\mathscrs{x})=\mathscrs{c}
, and where some vertices of
\mathscrs{x}
become activated.
We claim that during the entire RPP not too many such tuples become activated.
That is, we claim that

(6.52)

\displaystyle\omega\left(\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\left\{\mathscrs{x}\in X_{\sqcup I}^{H}\colon\phi_{r}(\mathscrs{x})=\mathscrs{c},\mathscrs{x}\cap Y_{\cup I}^{H}\neq\emptyset\right\}\right)\leq\mu^{1/2}\omega(\phi_{r}^{-1}(\mathscrs{c}))+n^{{\varepsilon}_{T}},

with high probability.
To see (
6.52
), note that for every
\mathscrs{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup I}
, the probability that
\mathscrs{x}
contains an activated vertex is at most
|I|\mu
.
Hence, an application of Theorem
3.1
and a union bound yield (
6.52
) with probability, say, at least
1-{\rm e}^{-n^{{\varepsilon}}}
.

Next, we consider tuples
\mathscrs{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}}
that have not been embedded onto
\mathscrs{c}
by
\phi_{r}
.
To that end, we fix
m\in[|I|]
and
J\in\binom{I}{m}
, and a pair of disjoint sets
J_{X},J_{V}\subseteq J
.
We aim to control the
\omega
-weight on the tuples in
E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{c},J,J_{X},J_{V})
as defined in (
6.28
).
Analogously as in Claim
6
, we can employ
\mathbf{S}(r)
(b)
and
(d)
for the edge testers that we defined for
(\omega,\mathscrs{c})
in (
6.5
).
Proceeding as in Claim
6
yields that

(6.53)

\displaystyle\omega\left(E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{c},J,J_{X},J_{V})\right)\leq\left(\mathbbm{1}\{J_{XV}=\emptyset\}+2{\varepsilon}_{T}\right)\prod_{i\in J}d_{i}\frac{\omega(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}})}{n^{|I|-|J|}}+n^{2{\varepsilon}_{T}}.

We can now proceed similarly as in the proof of Claim
6
in Step
6
; that is, we consider different cases and sets of tuples
\mathscrs{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}}
that could potentially be embedded onto
\mathscrs{c}
, and derive an upper bound on the expected
\omega
-weight used in such a case.
In the end, a union bound over all these cases gives us an upper bound on the total expected
\omega
-weight of tuples that are embedded onto
\mathscrs{c}
during the completion.
Therefore, we consider different choices for
J_{X},J_{V}\subseteq J
.

For
\mathscrs{x}=\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{c},J,J_{X}=\emptyset,J_{V}=\emptyset)\setminus E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{c},J,J_{X}=\emptyset,J_{V}\neq\emptyset)
, in order that
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
can be mapped onto
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in J}
during the RPP, it must hold that the vertices
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
and
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in J}
become activated because
J_{V}=\emptyset
.
Since
\phi_{r}(\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J})\neq\{c_{i}\}_{i\in J}
, this happens with probability at most
\mu^{m+1}
.
Further, by
(K)C
, the activated vertices
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
are mapped onto
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in J}
with probability at most
2^{m}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}
.
Altogether, this implies that the expected weight of edges in
E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{c},J,J_{X}=\emptyset,J_{V}=\emptyset)\setminus E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{c},J,J_{X}=\emptyset,J_{V}\neq\emptyset)
that are mapped onto
\mathscrs{c}
during the RPP is at most

\displaystyle\mu^{m+1}2^{m}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}\omega\left(E_{\phi_{r}}\left(\mathscrs{c},J,J_{X}=\emptyset,J_{V}=\emptyset\right)\right)

(6.54)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:weight leftover for g}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\mu^{m+1}2^{m}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}\left(2\prod_{i\in J}d_{i}\frac{\omega(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}})}{n^{|I|-|J|}}+n^{2{\varepsilon}_{T}}\right)\leq\mu^{4/5}\frac{\omega(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}})}{n^{|I|}}+n^{3{\varepsilon}_{T}}.

Next, we consider the set
E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{c},J,J_{X},J_{V})
for disjoint but fixed
J_{X},J_{V}\subseteq J
such that
J_{XV}=J_{X}\cup J_{V}\neq\emptyset
.
The vertices
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in J}
are mapped onto
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in J}
with probability at most
2^{m}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}
by
(K)C
.
Hence,
the expected weight of edges in
E_{\phi_{r}}(\mathscrs{c},J,J_{X},J_{V})
that are mapped onto
\mathscrs{c}
during the RPP is at most

(6.55)

\displaystyle 2^{m}\prod_{i\in J}(\mu d_{i}n)^{-1}\omega\left(E_{\phi_{r}}\left(\mathscrs{c},J,J_{X},J_{V}\right)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:weight leftover for g}}}{{\leq}}{\varepsilon}_{T}^{1/2}\frac{\omega(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}})}{n^{|I|}}+n^{3{\varepsilon}_{T}}.

Altogether, a union bound over all
m\in[|I|]
,
J\in\binom{I}{m}
, and all sets
J_{X},J_{V}\subseteq J
that we considered to be fixed in (
6.54
) and (
6.55
) together with an application of Lemma
3.2
yields that

\displaystyle\omega\left(\left\{\mathscrs{x}\in\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup I}\colon\phi_{r}(\mathscrs{x})\neq\mathscrs{c},\phi^{|\mathcal{H}|}(\mathscrs{x})=\mathscrs{c}\right\}\right)\leq\mu^{1/2}\frac{\omega(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}})}{n^{|I|}}+n^{4{\varepsilon}_{T}}

with probability at least, say,
1-{\rm e}^{-n^{2{\varepsilon}_{T}}}
.

Combining this with (
6.52
) yields

\displaystyle\omega((\phi^{|\mathcal{H}|})^{-1}(\mathscrs{c}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hphantom{\text{$\mathbf{S}(r)$\ref{vertex tester}}}}}{{=}}(1\pm\mu^{1/2})\omega(\phi_{r}^{-1}(\mathscrs{c}))\pm\mu^{1/2}\frac{\omega(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}})}{n^{|I|}}\pm 2n^{4{\varepsilon}_{T}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{$\mathbf{S}(r)$\ref{vertex tester}}}}{{=}}(1\pm\alpha)\frac{\omega(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}})}{n^{|I|}}\pm n^{\alpha}.

This establishes conclusion
(iii)
of Lemma
6.1
and completes the proof of Claim
6
.

-

Step 5.12.

Finishing the completion

As the RPP outputs
\phi^{|\mathcal{H}|}
with positive probability by Claim
6
, we obtain a packing
\phi:=\phi^{|\mathcal{H}|}
of
\mathcal{H}
into
G
which clearly satisfies conclusions
(i)
and
(iii)
of Lemma
6.1
.
Note that by the construction of
\phi^{|\mathcal{H}|}
, we have that
\phi|_{X_{i}^{H}}=\phi_{r}|_{X_{i}^{H}\setminus Y_{i}^{H}}\cup\phi^{H}|_{Y_{i}^{H}}
for all
H\in\mathcal{H}
,
i\in[r]
.
Since
|Y_{i}^{H}|=(1\pm{\varepsilon}_{T}^{1/2})\mu n
, we therefore merely modified
\phi_{r}
to obtain
\phi
and thus,
\mathbf{S}(r)
(f)
easily implies conclusion
(ii)
of Lemma
6.1
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
6.1
.

\square

7.
Proof of the main results

In this section we prove Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.6
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Our general approach is as follows.
Given a blow-up instance
(\mathcal{H},G,R,\mathcal{X},\mathcal{V})
, we refine the vertex partition
\mathcal{X}
of the graphs in
\mathcal{H}
using Lemma
3.9
and we randomly refine the vertex partition
\mathcal{V}
of
G
accordingly.
Afterwards we can apply Lemma
6.1
to obtain the required packing of
\mathcal{H}
into
G
.

Suppose
1/n\ll{\varepsilon}\ll 1/t\ll\beta\ll\alpha,1/k
for a new parameter
\beta
.
For each
(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}
with
W\subseteq V_{i}
,
i\in[r]
, and each
\ell\in[m]
, let
\omega_{Y_{\ell}}\colon\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}}X_{i}^{H}\to\{0,1\}
be such that
\omega_{Y_{\ell}}(x)=\mathbbm{1}{\{x\in Y_{\ell}\}}
, and let
\mathcal{W}_{Y}
be the set containing all those weight functions.
Further, for all
\mathscrs{r}\in E(R)
, let
\omega{r}\colon\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{\mathscrs{r}}}\to\{0,1\}
be defined by
\omega{r}(\mathscrs{x}):=\mathbbm{1}\{\mathscrs{x}\in E(\mathcal{H})\}
, and let
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
be the set containing all those weight functions.
We apply Lemma
3.9
to
\mathcal{H}
with weight functions
\{\omega\colon(\omega,\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}\}\cup\mathcal{W}_{Y}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
.
This yields a refined partition
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=(X_{i,j}^{H})_{H\in\mathcal{H},i\in[r],j\in[\beta^{-1}]}
of
\mathcal{H}
such that for all
H\in\mathcal{H}
and
i\in[r]
, the partitions
(X_{i,j}^{H})_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}
of
X_{i}^{H}
satisfy the conclusions
(i)
–
(iv)
of Lemma
3.9
.

Let
R^{\prime}
be the
k
-graph with vertex set
[r]\times[\beta^{-1}]
and edge set

\left\{\{(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in[k]}\colon\{i_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[k]}\in E(R),j_{\ell}\in[\beta^{-1}]\right\}.

Note that
\Delta(R^{\prime})\leq\alpha^{-1}\beta^{-(k-1)}
because
\Delta(R)\leq\alpha^{-1}
.
Let
n^{\prime}:=\beta n
.

Employing conclusion
(iv)
of Lemma
3.9
for the weight functions in
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}
implies for all
\{(i_{\ell},j_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in[k]}\in E(R^{\prime})
with
\mathscrs{r}:=\{i_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in[k]}\in E(R)
that

\displaystyle\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}e_{H}(X_{i_{1},j_{1}}^{H},\ldots,X_{i_{k},j_{k}}^{H})

\displaystyle\leq(1+{\varepsilon})\beta^{k}\omega{r}(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{\mathscrs{r}}})+n^{1+{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=(1+{\varepsilon})\beta^{k}e_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{\mathscrs{r}}})+n^{1+{\varepsilon}}\leq(1-\alpha/2)dn^{\prime k},

because by assumption,
e_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{\mathscrs{r}}})\leq(1-\alpha)dn^{k}
and
d\geq n^{-{\varepsilon}}
.

Further, note that Lemma
3.9
(i)
implies for each
H\in\mathcal{H}
that
H[X_{\cup\mathscrs{r}}^{H}]
is a matching if
\mathscrs{r}\in E(R^{\prime})
and empty if
\mathscrs{r}\in\binom{[r]\times[\beta^{-1}]}{k}\setminus E(R^{\prime})
.

According to the refinement
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}
, we claim that there exists a refined partition
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=(V_{i,j})_{i\in[r],j\in[\beta^{-1}]}
of
\mathcal{V}
, where
(V_{i,j})_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}
is a partition of
V_{i}
for every
i\in[r]
such that

(a)

|W\cap V_{i,j}|=\beta|W|\pm\beta^{3/2}n
for all
(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}
and
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
with
W\subseteq V_{i}
;

(b)

\mathscr{B}^{\prime}:=(\mathcal{H},G,R^{\prime},\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{V}^{\prime})
is an
({\varepsilon}^{1/2},t,d)
-typical,
\beta^{-k}
-bounded blow-up instance of size
(n^{\prime},k,\beta^{-1}r)
with
n^{\prime}=\beta n
.

The existence of such a partition
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
can be seen by a probabilistic argument.
For all
i\in[r]
and
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
, we take disjoint subset
V_{i,j}
of
V_{i}
of size exactly
|X_{i,j}^{H}|
uniformly and independently at random.
We analyse the probability that
(a)
or
(b)
are not satisfied.
To that end, we consider the slightly different random experiment where we assign for all
i\in[r]
, every vertex in
V_{i}
uniformly and independently at random to some
V_{i,j}
for
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
.
For this experiment and a fixed
i\in[r]
, we consider the bad events that
(a)
or
(b)
are not satisfied; in such a case, we say the experiment
fails
(in step
i
).
Standard properties of the multinomial distribution yield that
|V_{i,j}|=|X_{i,j}^{H}|
for all
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
,
H\in\mathcal{H}
with probability at least
\Omega(n^{-\beta^{-1}})
.
Hence, together with Theorem
3.1
and a union bound, this yields that the original experiment fails in step
i
with probability, say, at most
{\rm e}^{-n^{1/2}}
.
Since in fact we take
V_{i,j}
of size exactly
|X_{i,j}^{H}|
,
this altogether implies the existence of a refined partition
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{V}
satisfying
(a)
and
(b)
with positive probability.

We show how to adapt the vertex and set testers from the original blow-up instance to the blow-up instance
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
.
For each
(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}
and distinct
H_{1},\ldots,H_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
W\subseteq V_{i}
for some
i\in[r]
and
Y_{\ell}\subseteq X_{i}^{H_{\ell}}
for all
\ell\in[m]
,
we define
(W_{j},Y_{1,j},\ldots,Y_{m,j})
by setting
W_{j}:=W\cap V_{i,j}
and
Y_{\ell,j}:=Y_{\ell}\cap X_{i,j}^{H_{\ell}}
for all
j\in[\beta^{-1}],\ell\in[m]
.
By
(a)
, we conclude that
|W_{j}|=\beta|W|\pm\beta^{3/2}n
.
Employing conclusion
(iii)
of Lemma
3.9
for the weight function
\omega_{Y_{\ell}}\in\mathcal{W}_{Y}
, we have that

(7.1)

\displaystyle|Y_{\ell,j}|=\omega_{Y_{\ell}}(X_{i,j}^{H_{\ell}})=\beta\omega_{Y_{\ell}}(X_{i}^{H_{\ell}})\pm\beta^{3/2}n=\beta|Y_{\ell}|\pm\beta^{3/2}n.

Let
\mathcal{W}_{set}^{\prime}:=\{(W_{j},Y_{1,j},\ldots,Y_{m,j})\colon j\in[\beta^{-1}],(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}\}
.
For each
(\omega,\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}
with centres
\mathscrs{c}=\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
I\subseteq[r]
and multiset
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq[\beta^{-1}]
such that
c_{i}\in V_{i,j_{i}}
for each
i\in I
, let

\omega^{\prime}:=\omega|_{\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H},i\in I}X_{i,j_{i}}^{H}}
and
\mathcal{W}_{ver}^{\prime}:=\{(\omega^{\prime},\mathscrs{c})\colon(\omega,\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}\}
.

Hence, we can apply Lemma
6.1
to
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
with set testers
\mathcal{W}_{set}^{\prime}
and vertex testers
\mathcal{W}_{ver}^{\prime}
as follows:

parameter

n^{\prime}

{\varepsilon}^{1/2}

t

\beta^{k}

d

r\beta^{-1}

plays the role of

n

{\varepsilon}

t

\alpha

d

r

This yields a packing of
\mathcal{H}
into
G
such that

(I)

\phi(X_{i,j}^{H})=V_{i,j}
for all
i\in[r]
,
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
,
H\in\mathcal{H}
;

(II)

|W_{j}\cap\bigcap_{\ell\in[m]}\phi(Y_{\ell,j})|=|W_{j}||Y_{1,j}|\cdots|Y_{m,j}|/n^{\prime m}\pm{\beta^{k}}n^{\prime}
for all
(W_{j},Y_{1,j},\ldots,Y_{\ell,j})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}^{\prime}
;

(III)

\omega^{\prime}(\phi^{-1}(\mathscrs{c})))=(1\pm\beta^{k})\omega^{\prime}(\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}}(\bigsqcup_{i\in I}X_{i,j_{i}}^{H}))/n^{\prime|I|}\pm n^{\prime\beta^{k}}
for all
(\omega^{\prime},\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}^{\prime}
with centres
\mathscrs{c}=\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
I\subseteq[r]
and multiset
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq[\beta^{-1}]
such that
c_{i}\in V_{i,j_{i}}
for each
i\in I
.

For
(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\textstyle\left|W\cap\bigcap_{\ell\in[m]}\phi(Y_{\ell})\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hphantom{\rm\ref{item:II},\ref{item:refined W},\eqref{eq:size Y_l,j}}}}{{=}}\sum_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}\textstyle\left|W_{j}\cap\bigcap_{\ell\in[m]}\phi(Y_{\ell,j})\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm\ref{item:II},\ref{item:refined W},\eqref{eq:size Y_l,j}}}{{=}}\sum_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}\left(\frac{\beta^{m+1}\left(|W||Y_{1}|\cdots|Y_{m}|\pm\beta^{1/3}n^{m+1}\right)}{(\beta n)^{m}}\pm{\beta^{k}}n^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hphantom{\rm\ref{item:II},\ref{item:refined W},\eqref{eq:size Y_l,j}}}}{{=}}|W||Y_{1}|\cdots|Y_{m}|/n^{m}\pm\alpha n.

This establishes Theorem
1.5
(i)
.

In order to establish Theorem
1.5
(ii)
, we fix
(\omega,\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}
with centres
\mathscrs{c}=\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
for
I\subseteq[r]
and multiset
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq[\beta^{-1}]
such that
c_{i}\in V_{i,j_{i}}
for each
i\in I
, and we fix the corresponding tuple
(\omega^{\prime},\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}^{\prime}
.
We conclude that

\displaystyle\omega(\phi^{-1}(\mathscrs{c}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm\ref{item:I}}}{{=}}\penalty\ \omega^{\prime}(\phi^{-1}(\mathscrs{c}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm\ref{item:III}}}{{=}}(1\pm\beta^{k})\frac{\omega^{\prime}\left(\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\bigsqcup_{i\in I}X_{i,j_{i}}^{H}\right)\right)}{n^{\prime|I|}}\pm n^{\prime\beta^{k}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hphantom{\rm\ref{item:split weights}}}}{{=}}(1\pm\beta^{k})\frac{\omega\left(\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\bigsqcup_{i\in I}X_{i,j_{i}}^{H}\right)\right)}{n^{\prime|I|}}\pm n^{\prime\beta^{k}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm\ref{item:split weights}}}}{{=}}(1\pm\beta^{k})\frac{(1\pm{\varepsilon})\beta^{|I|}\omega(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}})\pm n^{1+{\varepsilon}}}{(\beta n)^{|I|}}\pm n^{\prime\beta^{k}}=(1\pm\alpha)\omega(\mathscr{X}_{\sqcup{I}})/n^{|I|}\pm n^{\alpha},

where we employed conclusion
(iv)
of Lemma
3.9
in the penultimate equation.
This establishes Theorem
1.5
(ii)
and completes the proof.

\square

We now proceed to the proof of Theorem
1.6
.
The highlevel strategy is similar as in the proof of Theorem
1.5
.
Additionally, we group the hypergraphs in
\mathcal{H}
into
P=\plog n
many collections of hypergraphs
\mathcal{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{P}
and accordingly we partition the edge set of the host graph
G
into
G_{1},\ldots,G_{P}
subgraphs.
Afterwards, we partition the vertex sets of the graphs in
\mathcal{H}
via Lemma
3.10
and randomly partition the vertex set of each
G_{p}
for
p\in[P]
accordingly.
Then, we can iteratively apply Lemma
6.1
for each
p\in[P]
to map
\mathcal{H}_{p}
into
G_{p}
.
Note that this yields a packing of
\mathcal{H}
into
G
, and
considering
P=\plog n
partitions enables us to establish conclusions
(i)
and
(ii)
of Theorem
1.6
.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

We set
P:=\log^{t}n
and suppose
1/n\ll{\varepsilon}\ll 1/t\ll\beta\ll\alpha,1/k
for a new parameter
\beta
.

First, we group the graphs in
\mathcal{H}
into
P
collections
\mathcal{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{P}
with roughly equally many edges.
That is, we claim that there exists a partition of
\mathcal{H}
into
P
collections of graphs
\mathcal{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{P}
such that each
H\in\mathcal{H}
belongs to exactly one
\mathcal{H}_{p}
for
p\in[P]
, and for each
p\in[P]
, we have

(7.2)

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{H}_{p})\leq(1+{\varepsilon})P^{-1}e(\mathcal{H})+n^{1+{\varepsilon}},

and for every
\omega\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}
with
\omega(V(\mathcal{H}))\geq n^{1+{\varepsilon}}
and each
p\in[P]
, we have

(7.3)

\displaystyle\omega(V(\mathcal{H}_{p}))=(1\pm{\varepsilon})P^{-1}\omega(V(\mathcal{H})).

The existence of
\mathcal{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{P}
can be easily seen by assigning every graph
H\in\mathcal{H}
to one collection
\mathcal{H}_{p}
for
p\in[P]
uniformly and independently at random.

We now aim to apply Lemma
3.10
to each
\mathcal{H}_{p}
.
Let
p\in[P]
be fixed.
For each
(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}
and each
\ell\in[m]
such that
Y_{\ell}\subseteq V(H_{\ell})
for
H_{\ell}\in\mathcal{H}_{p}
, let
\omega_{Y_{\ell}}\colon V(H_{\ell})\to\{0,1\}
be such that
\omega_{Y_{\ell}}(x)=\mathbbm{1}{\{x\in Y_{\ell}\}}
, and let
\mathcal{W}_{Y}
be the set containing all those weight functions.
Further, let
\omega_{edges}\colon\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\binom{V(H)}{k}_{\prec}\to\{0,1\}
be defined by
\omega_{edges}((x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})):=\mathbbm{1}\{\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}\in E(\mathcal{H}_{p})\}
, and let
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}
be the set containing all those weight functions.
We apply Lemma
3.10
to
\mathcal{H}_{p}
with weight functions
\{\omega|_{\cup V(\mathcal{H}_{p})}\colon(\omega,\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}\}\cup\mathcal{W}_{Y}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}
.
This yields a partition
\mathcal{X}_{p}=(X_{j}^{H})_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{p},j\in[\beta^{-1}]}
of
\mathcal{H}_{p}
such that for all
H\in\mathcal{H}_{p}
, the partitions
(X_{j}^{H})_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}
of
V(H)
satisfy the conclusions
(i)
–
(iv)
of Lemma
3.10
.

For each
p\in[P]
, let
R_{p}
be the
k
-graph with vertex set
[\beta^{-1}]
and
\mathscrs{r}\in\binom{[\beta^{-1}]}{k}
is an edge in
R_{p}
if
H[X_{\sqcup\mathscrs{r}}^{H}]
is non-empty for some
H\in\mathcal{H}_{p}
.
Clearly,
\Delta(R_{p})\leq\beta^{-k}
.
Let
n^{\prime}:=\beta n
.

Employing conclusion
(iv)
of Lemma
3.10
for the weight functions in
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}_{p}}
yields for all
\mathscrs{r}=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\in E(R_{p})
that

\displaystyle\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{p}}e_{H}(X_{\sqcup\mathscrs{r}}^{H})

\displaystyle=\omega_{edges}(\textstyle\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{p}}(X_{i_{1}}^{H}\times\ldots\times X_{i_{k}}^{H}))\leq(1+\beta^{1/2})\beta^{k}\omega_{edges}(V(\mathcal{H}_{p}))+n^{1+{\varepsilon}}

(7.4)

\displaystyle=(1+\beta^{1/2})\beta^{k}k!e(\mathcal{H}_{p})+n^{1+{\varepsilon}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:edges sliced Hs}}}{{\leq}}(1-\alpha/2)P^{-1}dn^{\prime k}

because by assumption,
e(\mathcal{H})\leq(1-\alpha)e(G)
.

Now, we want to prepare
G
accordingly to
\mathcal{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{P}
and their partitions.
To that end, we first partition
G
into
P
edge-disjoint spanning subgraphs
G_{1},\ldots,G_{P}
such that
G_{p}
is
({\varepsilon}^{1/2},t,P^{-1}d)
-typical for every
p\in[P]
.
The existence of
G_{1},\ldots,G_{P}
can be seen by assigning every edge in
G
to one subgraph
G_{p}
for
p\in[P]
uniformly and independently at random.

Further, we claim that there exist partitions
\mathcal{V}_{p}=(V_{j})_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}
of
V(G_{p})
according to the partition
\mathcal{X}_{p}
of
\mathcal{H}_{p}
, such that for every
p\in[P]
and
\mathcal{V}_{p}=(V_{j})_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}
, we have

(a)

|W\cap V_{j}|=\beta|W|\pm\beta^{3/2}n
for all
(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}
and
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
,

(b)

\mathscr{B}_{p}:=(\mathcal{H}_{p},G_{p},R_{p},\mathcal{X}_{p},\mathcal{V}_{p})
is an
({\varepsilon}^{1/2},t,P^{-1}d)
-typical,
\beta^{-k}
-bounded blow-up instance of size
(n^{\prime},k,\beta^{-1})
with
n^{\prime}=\beta n
,

as well as

(c)

\sum_{p\in[P]\colon\text{centres collide}}\omega(V(\mathcal{H}_{p}))\leq\beta^{1/2}\omega(V(\mathcal{H}))
for all
(\omega,\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}
with
\omega(V(\mathcal{H}))\geq n^{1+{\varepsilon}}
, where we say that the
centres
\mathscrs{c}
collide (with respect to
\mathcal{V}_{p}
)
if
|\mathscrs{c}\cap V_{j}|\geq 2
for some
V_{j}\in\mathcal{V}_{p}
.

The existence of such partitions
\mathcal{V}_{p}
can be seen by assigning for every
p\in[P]
, every vertex to some
V_{j}
for
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
uniformly and independently at random.
Theorem
3.1
and a union bound establish
(a)
with probability, say, at least
1-{\rm e}^{-n^{1/2}}
.
For
(b)
, note that standard properties of the multinomial distribution yield for
p\in[P]
that
|V_{j}|=|X_{j}^{H}|
for all
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
and
H\in\mathcal{H}
with probability at least
\Omega(n^{-\beta^{-1}})
.
For
(c)
, note that for
p\in[P]
, the probability that the centres
\mathscrs{c}
collide with respect to
\mathcal{V}_{p}
is at most
k^{2}\beta
.
By (
7.3
), we therefore expect that at most

\sum_{p\in[P]}k^{2}\beta\omega(V(\mathcal{H}_{p}))\leq 2k^{2}\beta\omega(V(\mathcal{H}))

weight of
\omega
collides in
(c)
.
Since
P
grows sufficiently fast in terms of
n
, we can establish concentration. That is, Theorem
3.1
and a union bound that yield
(c)
with probability, say, at least
1-n^{-\log n}
.
Hence, a final union bound yields the existence of these partitions
\mathcal{V}_{p}
for
p\in[P]
satisfying
(a)
–
(c)
with positive probability.

Next, we iteratively apply Lemma
6.1
to
\mathscr{B}_{p}
for
p\in[P]
which yields a packing
\phi_{p}
of
\mathcal{H}_{p}
into
G_{p}
.
Let us first explain how we adapt the vertex and set testers from the original blow-up instance to the blow-up instance
\mathscr{B}_{p}
.

For
p\in[P]
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{ver}(p):=\Big\{(\omega_{p},\mathscrs{c})\colon

\displaystyle(\omega,\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver},\text{ centres $\mathscrs{c}=\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}$ do not collide with respect to $\mathcal{V}_{p}$,}

\displaystyle\omega_{p}:=\omega|_{\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{p},i\in I}X_{j_{i}}^{H}}\Big\},

where we define
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq[\beta^{-1}]
as the indices such that
c_{i}\in V_{j_{i}}
for centres
\mathscrs{c}=\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
that do not collide with respect to
\mathcal{V}_{p}=(V_{j})_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}
.

For all
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
,
(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}
and distinct
H_{1},\ldots,H_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
Y_{\ell}\subseteq V(H_{\ell})
, we define
Y_{\ell,j}:=Y_{\ell}\cap X_{j}^{H_{\ell}}
for each
\ell\in[m]
.
For
j\in[\beta^{-1}],p\in[P]
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{set}(j,p):=\Big\{\big(W_{j}(p),\{Y_{\ell,j}\}_{\ell\in[m]\colon Y_{\ell,j}\subseteq V(\mathcal{H}_{p})}\big)\colon(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set},\penalty\ Y_{\cup[m]}\cap V(\mathcal{H}_{p})\neq\emptyset\Big\},

where we define
W_{j}(p)
for
(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}
recursively by

W_{j}(p):=W_{j}(p-1)\cap\bigcap_{\ell\in[m]\colon Y_{\ell,j}\subseteq V(\mathcal{H}_{p-1})}\phi_{p-1}(Y_{\ell,j})

with
\phi_{0}
being the empty function and thus,
W_{j}(1)=W_{j}(0):=W\cap V_{j}
.
By
(a)
, we have that
|W\cap V_{j}|=\beta|W|\pm\beta^{3/2}n
.
By employing conclusion
(iii)
of Lemma
3.10
for the weight function
\omega_{Y_{\ell}}\in\mathcal{W}_{Y}
, we have that

(7.5)

\displaystyle|Y_{\ell,j}|=\omega_{Y_{\ell}}(X_{j}^{H_{\ell}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{item:split' weights one H, |I|=1}}}{{=}}\beta\omega_{Y_{\ell}}(V({H_{\ell}}))\pm\beta^{3/2}n=\beta|Y_{\ell}|\pm\beta^{3/2}n.

Hence, we iteratively apply Lemma
6.1
for every
p\in[P]
to
\mathscr{B}_{p}
with set testers
\bigcup_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}\mathcal{W}_{set}(j,p)
and vertex testers
\mathcal{W}_{ver}(p)
as follows:

parameter

n^{\prime}

{\varepsilon}^{1/2}

t

\beta^{k}

P^{-1}d

\beta^{-1}

plays the role of

n

{\varepsilon}

t

\alpha

d

r

For every
p\in[P]
, this yields a packing
\phi_{p}
of
\mathcal{H}_{p}
into
G_{p}
such that

(I)

\phi_{p}(X_{j}^{H})=V_{j}
for all
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
,
H\in\mathcal{H}_{p}
;

(II)

|W_{j}(p)\cap\bigcap_{\ell\in[m]\colon Y_{\ell,j}\subseteq V(\mathcal{H}_{p})}\phi_{p}(Y_{\ell,j})|=|W_{j}(p)|\prod_{\ell\in[m]\colon Y_{\ell,j}\subseteq V(\mathcal{H}_{p})}n^{\prime-1}|Y_{\ell,j}|\pm\beta^{k}n^{\prime}

for all
(W_{j}(p),\{Y_{\ell,j}\}_{\ell\in[m]\colon Y_{\ell}\subseteq V(\mathcal{H}_{p})})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}(j,p)
,
j\in[\beta^{-1}]
;

(III)

\omega_{p}(\phi_{p}^{-1}(\mathscrs{c})))=(1\pm\beta^{k})\omega_{p}(\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{p}}(\bigsqcup_{i\in I}X_{j_{i}}^{H}))/n^{\prime|I|}\pm n^{\prime\beta^{k}}
for all
(\omega_{p},\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}(p)
with centres
\mathscrs{c}=\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
that do not collide with respect to
\mathcal{V}_{p}
and
\{j_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq[\beta^{-1}]
such that
c_{i}\in V_{j_{i}}
for each
i\in I
.

Let
\phi:=\bigcup_{p\in[P]}\phi_{p}
and note that
\phi
is a packing of
\mathcal{H}
into
G
.

For
(W,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{set}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\textstyle\left|W\cap\bigcap_{\ell\in[m]}\phi(Y_{\ell})\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hphantom{\rm\ref{item:refined W},\eqref{eq:size Y_l,j r=1}}}}{{=}}\sum_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}\textstyle\left|W_{j}(P)\cap\bigcap_{\ell\in[m]\colon Y_{\ell,j}\subseteq V(\mathcal{H}_{P})}\phi_{P}(Y_{\ell,j})\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm\penalty\ \penalty\ \ref{item:II r=1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ }}{{=}}\sum_{j\in[\beta^{-1}]}\left(|W_{j}(0)||Y_{1,j}|\cdots|Y_{m,j}|/n^{\prime m}\pm m\beta^{k}n^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm\ref{item:refined W r=1},\eqref{eq:size Y_l,j r=1}}}{{=}}|W||Y_{1}|\cdots|Y_{m}|/n^{m}\pm\alpha n.

This establishes Theorem
1.6
(i)
.

In order to establish Theorem
1.6
(ii)
, we fix
(\omega,\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}
with centres
\mathscrs{c}=\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
.
Recall that
{\rm supp}(\omega)\subseteq E(\mathcal{H})
for
|I|\geq 2
, and thus, if the centres
\mathscrs{c}
collide with respect to one of the partitions
\mathcal{V}_{p}
, then
\omega(\phi_{p}^{-1}(\mathscrs{c}))=0
.
Therefore, we can consider the corresponding tuples
(\omega_{p},\mathscrs{c})\in\mathcal{W}_{ver}(p)
for
p\in[P]
and conclude that

\displaystyle\omega(\phi^{-1}(\mathscrs{c}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hphantom{\rm\ref{item:III r=1}}}}{{=}}\sum_{p\in[P]}\omega(\phi_{p}^{-1}(\mathscrs{c}))=\sum_{p\in[P]\colon\text{no collision}}\omega_{p}(\phi_{p}^{-1}(\mathscrs{c}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm\ref{item:III r=1}}}{{=}}\sum_{p\in[P]\colon\text{no collision}}\left((1\pm\beta^{k})\frac{\omega_{p}(\bigcup_{H\in\mathcal{H}_{p}}(\bigsqcup_{i\in I}X_{j_{i}}^{H}))}{n^{\prime|I|}}\pm n^{\prime\beta^{k}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm\ref{item:split' weights}}}{{=}}\sum_{p\in[P]\colon\text{no collision}}\left((1\pm\beta^{k})\frac{(1\pm\beta^{1/2})\beta^{|I|}\omega(V(\mathcal{H}_{p}))}{n^{\prime|I|}}\pm n^{\prime\beta^{k}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\penalty\ \rm\ref{item:centres collide}\penalty\ }}{{=}}(1\pm\alpha)\frac{\omega(V(\mathcal{H}))}{n^{|I|}}\pm n^{\alpha}.

This establishes Theorem
1.6
(ii)
and completes the proof.

\square

8.
Concluding remarks

In this paper we have proved a functional tool that allows for approximate decompositions of (sparse) (multipartite) quasirandom
k
-graphs into any collection of spanning bounded degree
k
-graphs (with slightly fewer edges).
Our main result extends the strong decomposition result due to Kim, Kühn, Osthus and Tyomkyn into two directions, both from the dense to the sparse regime and from graphs to hypergraphs.
It thereby answers Question
1.1
of Keevash, and Kim, Kühn, Osthus and Tyomkyn
and Question
1.2
of Kim, Kühn, Osthus and Tyomkyn.

We conclude with a selection of immediate applications of Theorem
1.3

including an asymptotic solution to a hypergraph Oberwolfach problem asked by Glock, Kühn and Osthus
[
21
]
.
We first consider several natural hypergraph analogues of graph decomposition questions and then turn to problems concerning simplicial complexes. Along the way we state a few conjectures and open problems. We also believe that our main results will have further significantly more complex applications in the future.

8.1.
Applications to hypergraph decompositions

As we pointed out in the introduction,
the Conjectures
(I)
–
(III)
intrigued mathematicians for decades.
In the following we propose several conjectures of similar spirit for
k
-graphs.
Our main results imply approximate versions thereof.

Recall that the Oberwolfach problem in
(III)
asks for a decomposition of
K_{n}
into
(n-1)/2
copies of a graph on
n
vertices that is the disjoint union of cycles.
There are many definitions for cycles in
k
-graphs and tight cycles are among the most well studied cycles.
A
k
-graph is a tight cycle if its vertex set can be cyclically ordered and the edge set consists of all
k
-sets that appear consecutively in this ordering.
We refer to the number of vertices in a tight cycle as its length.
One potential version of a hypergraph Oberwolfach problem has recently been asked by Glock, Kühn and Osthus.

Conjecture 8.1

(Hypergraph Oberwolfach problem; Glock, Kühn and Osthus
[
21
]
)
.

Let
k\geq 3
and suppose
n
is sufficiently large in terms of
k
and
k
divides
\binom{n-1}{k-1}
.
Suppose
F
is a
k
-graph on
n
vertices that is the disjoint union of tight cycles each of length at least
2k-1
.
Then there is a decomposition of
K_{n}^{(k)}
into copies of
F
.

Clearly, Theorem
1.3
yields an approximate solution of Conjecture
8.1
.

We think that an even stronger result is true.

Conjecture 8.2

(Hypergraph Oberwolfach problem)
.

Let
k\geq 3
and suppose
n
is sufficiently large in terms of
k
and
k
divides
\binom{n-1}{k-1}
.
Suppose
F
is a
k
-graph on
n
vertices that is the disjoint union of tight cycles each of length at least
k+2
.
Then there is a decomposition of
K_{n}^{(k)}
into copies of
F
.

Observe that Conjecture
8.1
includes the natural generalization of Walecki’s theorem to hypergraphs, namely decompositions into Hamilton cycles.
This has already been conjectured by Bailey and Stevens
[
6
]
(and when
n
and
k
are coprime by Baranyai
[
8
]
and independently by Katona) and there are a few results that provide approximate decompositions of quasirandom graphs into Hamilton cycles (of various types); see for example
[
7
,
16
,
17
]
.

Whenever we allow cycles of length
k+1
and the cycle factor consists (essentially) of cycles of length
k+1
, we suspect that there are more divisibility obstructions present. Hence we pose the following problem.

Problem 8.3

(Hypergraph Oberwolfach problem)
.

Let
k\geq 3
and suppose
n
is sufficiently large in terms of
k
and
k
divides
\binom{n-1}{k-1}
.
Which disjoint unions of tight cycles whose length add up to
n
admit a decomposition of
K_{n}^{(k)}
?

It immediately follows from Theorem
1.3
that the hypergraph Oberwolfach problems are approximately true in a sense that
K_{n}^{(k)}
contains
(1-o(1))\binom{n-1}{k-1}/k
disjoint copies of
F
(for any choice of
F
as above);
in fact, we can we take any collection of
(1-o(1))\binom{n-1}{k-1}/k
cycle factors.

Similarly as for cycles, there is more then one notion for trees in
k
-graphs.
Let us stick to the following recursive definition of tree to which we refer as a
k
-tree.
A single edge is a
k
-tree.
A
k
-tree with
\ell
edges can be constructed from a
k
-tree with
\ell-1
edges
T
by adding a vertex
v
and an edge that contains
v
and a
(k-1)
-set that is contained in an edge of
T
.
For this definition, we propose the following generalization of Ringel’s conjecture.

Conjecture 8.4
.

Let
k,n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}
.
Suppose
T
is a
k
-tree with
n
edges.
Then
K_{kn+k-1}^{(k)}
admits a decomposition into copies of
T
.

Observe that similarly as for Ringel’s conjecture, the order of the complete graph needs to be at least
kn+k-1
if we allow
T
to be any tree with
n
edges as the natural generalization of a star shows.
It is an easy exercise to show this conjecture for stars.

There is a conjecture related to Ringel’s conjecture for bipartite graphs due to Graham and Häggkvist
stating that
K_{n,n}
can be decomposed into
n
copies of any tree with
n
edges.
We propose here the following strengthening.

Conjecture 8.5
.

Suppose
k,n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}
and
T
is a
k
-tree with
n
edges.
Then there is a decomposition of the complete balanced
k
-partite graph on
kn
vertices.

The tree packing conjecture has arguably the least obvious strengthening to
k
-graphs
and there may be more than one.
We propose the following one.

Conjecture 8.6
.

Suppose
k,n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{1\}
.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be a family of
k
-trees such that
\mathcal{T}
contains
\binom{n-i-1}{k-2}
trees with
i
edges for
i\in[n-k+1]
.
Then
K_{n}^{(k)}
admits a decomposition into
\mathcal{T}
.

It follows directly from Theorem
1.3
that Conjectures
8.4
and
8.6
are approximately true when restricted to bounded degree trees (and similarly an approximate version of Conjecture
8.5
follows from our main theorem, see Theorem
1.4
in Section
1.3
).

8.2.
Applications to simplicial complexes

Generalizing long-studied and nowadays classical combinatorial questions to higher dimensions appears to be a challenging but insightful theme.
There are several results considering
k
-dimensional permutations
and it was Linial and Meshulam
[
33
]
who introduced a random model for simplicial complexes whose probability measure is the same as those of a binomial random
(d+1)
-graph; simply add all
d^{\prime}
-faces for
d^{\prime}\leq d-1
with points in
[n]
and add every potential
d
-face independently with probability
p
.
Recently, Linial and Peled investigated and determined the threshold in
Y_{d}(n,p)
for the emergence of a what may considered as an analogoue of a giant component
[
34
]
.

With this topological viewpoint of treating a
k
-graph as a simplicial complex,
a cycle in a graph is simply an object homomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{1}

and hence a
2
-dimensional Hamilton cycle a collection of
2
-faces containing every vertex and which is homomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
In
[
35
]
, Luria and Tessler determined the threshold in
Y_{2}(n,p)
for the appearance of such a Hamilton cycle (another suitable term may be spanning triangulation of the sphere).

An analogue of Dirac’s theorem was proved by Georgakopoulos, Haslegrave, Narayanan and Montgomery;
to be precise, when every pair of vertices is contained in at least
n/3+o(n)
edges/2-faces of a
3
-graph
G
, then there is a spanning triangulation of the sphere in
G
.
This bound remains the same when we replace
\mathbb{S}^{2}
by any other compact surface without boundary
[
18
]
.

Instead of only asking for a single triangulation of some surface, we can of course also investigate decompositions into (spanning) triangulations of surfaces.
Our results imply that every quasirandom simplicial complex (this in particular includes almost all graphs in
Y_{d}(n,p)
) can even be almost decomposed into any list of triangulations of any kind of manifolds provided the density does not vanish too quickly with
n
and as long as every vertex is contained in at most a bounded number of
d
-faces.
The triangulations may even be chosen in advance.
Hence a precise statement for 2-complexes is as follows.

Corollary 8.7
.

For all
\alpha>0
,
there exist
n_{0},h\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\varepsilon}>0
such that the following holds for all
n\geq n_{0}
.
Suppose
G
is an
({\varepsilon},h,d)
-typical
3
-graph on
n
vertices with
d\geq n^{-{\varepsilon}}
and

H_{1},\ldots,H_{\ell}
are spanning triangulations of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
where every vertex is contained in at most
\alpha^{-1}

2
-faces and
\ell\leq(1-\alpha)dn^{2}/12
.
Then
G
contains edge-disjoint copies of
H_{1},\ldots,H_{\ell}
such that every
2
-face of
G
is contained in at most one
H_{i}
.

We omit statements for higher dimensions as they follow in the obvious way from Theorem
1.3
.
We wonder whether there is always an actual decomposition (subject to certain divisibility conditions).
This might be easier than a decomposition into tight Hamilton cycles as the structure of tight Hamilton cycles seems to be more restrictive.
